### Multihead self-attention in cortico-thalamic circuits
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.06354v1)
- **Authors**: Arno Granier, Walter Senn
- **Abstract**: Both biological cortico-thalamic networks and artificial transformer networks use canonical computations to perform a wide range of cognitive tasks. In this work, we propose that the structure of cortico-thalamic circuits is well suited to realize a computation analogous to multihead self-attention, the main algorithmic innovation of transformers. We start with the concept of a cortical unit module or microcolumn, and propose that superficial and deep pyramidal cells carry distinct computational roles. Specifically, superficial pyramidal cells encode an attention mask applied onto deep pyramidal cells to compute attention-modulated values. We show how to wire such microcolumns into a circuit equivalent to a single head of self-attention. We then suggest the parallel between one head of attention and a cortical area. On this basis, we show how to wire cortico-thalamic circuits to perform multihead self-attention. Along these constructions, we refer back to existing experimental data, and find noticeable correspondence. Finally, as a first step towards a mechanistic theory of synaptic learning in this framework, we derive formal gradients of a tokenwise mean squared error loss for a multihead linear self-attention block.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a neurobiological implementation of multihead self-attention (MHSA), a core mechanism in transformer networks, within cortico-thalamic circuits. The authors map the key components of MHSA (keys, queries, values, attention mechanism) onto specific neuronal structures and cell types within the cortex and thalamus. They suggest superficial and deep pyramidal cells in cortical microcolumns fulfill distinct computational roles related to attention, and show how these microcolumns can be wired to implement a single attention head. The authors then propose that cortical areas can be viewed as analogous to attention heads and describe how cortico-thalamic loops could realize multihead self-attention. Finally, they derive formal gradients for learning in such a model, which enables the formulation of synaptic learning rules within this neurobiological framework. The authors highlight connections to existing experimental data to support their proposal.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its specific mapping of MHSA to detailed neural circuitry. While previous work has hinted at brain-like computations in transformers and transformer-like computations in neural circuits, this paper provides a more concrete and detailed mapping, linking algorithmic elements to specific cell types and pathways within the cortico-thalamic system. The suggestion of a direct parallel between a cortical area and an attention head is also a novel and interesting proposal. The derivation of learning gradients, though standard in machine learning, is novel in this context, providing a potential pathway for developing biologically plausible learning rules in the model.

**Significance:** The significance stems from several factors:

*   **Bridging AI and Neuroscience:**  It offers a concrete example of how a powerful AI concept (MHSA) might be implemented in the brain. This is valuable for both AI researchers seeking biologically plausible architectures and neuroscientists seeking computational interpretations of brain circuitry.
*   **New Interpretations of Neural Function:** The mapping suggests new ways to interpret the roles of different cell types and pathways, prompting new experimental hypotheses.
*   **Towards Biologically Plausible Learning:** The derived gradients offer a starting point for developing local, biologically plausible learning rules that could enable these networks to learn and adapt.

However, the paper also has limitations:

*   **Speculative Nature:** The mapping is primarily theoretical and speculative. While the authors cite experimental data to support their ideas, the paper is largely based on analogy and interpretation rather than direct evidence. It needs to be experimentally validated.
*   **Simplifications and Abstractions:** The model involves simplifications of real neural circuitry. The specific implementation of the attention mechanism (linear self-attention) is a simplification of the more common softmax version and may not fully capture the complexity of neural computation.
*   **Quadratic Scaling Problem:** As acknowledged by the authors, the naive quadratic scaling of transformers creates biological implausibility with respect to the number of neurons. The proposed solution is linear self-attention, which requires an approximation to work.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper scores a **7**.

Here's why:

*   **+2 points for Novelty**: It introduces a novel and detailed mapping between the abstract concept of multi-head self-attention and the concrete implementation of cortico-thalamic circuits, including specific cell types and their interactions. The application of multihead attention from NLP to cortical models is an original and intriguing connection.
*   **+2 points for Significance**: It provides a potentially valuable framework for understanding information processing in the brain, linking AI concepts with neurobiological mechanisms and suggesting testable hypotheses.
*   **+2 point for impact**: It makes use of experimental data to support its claims, strengthening the case. Derivation of Learning gradients for backpropagation in these circuits is impactful.
*   **-1 points for lack of validation**: Validation is entirely theoretical, which means a real validation in neuronal circuits is absent.
*   **-2 point for theoretical limitations**: Certain simplifications and assumptions weaken the model's biological plausibility and the limited quadratic scaling factor of transformers. The assumption of core and matrix connectivity (Jones, 1998) are not always followed in practice.

In summary, the paper is a valuable contribution due to its novel framework and potential for generating new insights. However, the inherent limitations of its speculative nature and the need for experimental validation limit its immediate impact and justify the score of 7.

### Query Understanding in LLM-based Conversational Information Seeking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.06356v1)
- **Authors**: Yifei Yuan, Zahra Abbasiantaeb, Yang Deng, Mohammad Aliannejadi
- **Abstract**: Query understanding in Conversational Information Seeking (CIS) involves accurately interpreting user intent through context-aware interactions. This includes resolving ambiguities, refining queries, and adapting to evolving information needs. Large Language Models (LLMs) enhance this process by interpreting nuanced language and adapting dynamically, improving the relevance and precision of search results in real-time. In this tutorial, we explore advanced techniques to enhance query understanding in LLM-based CIS systems. We delve into LLM-driven methods for developing robust evaluation metrics to assess query understanding quality in multi-turn interactions, strategies for building more interactive systems, and applications like proactive query management and query reformulation. We also discuss key challenges in integrating LLMs for query understanding in conversational search systems and outline future research directions. Our goal is to deepen the audience's understanding of LLM-based conversational query understanding and inspire discussions to drive ongoing advancements in this field.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper is a tutorial outline on "Query Understanding in LLM-based Conversational Information Seeking (CIS)." It aims to provide an overview of advanced techniques for enhancing query understanding in conversational search systems that leverage Large Language Models (LLMs). The tutorial covers topics such as evaluating query understanding, improving conversational interaction with LLMs, proactive query management strategies, and query enhancement techniques. It also touches upon open challenges and future research directions, specifically focusing on multilingual support and real-time adaptation to evolving user intent. The intended audience includes researchers, students, and practitioners interested in the intersection of LLMs, conversational AI, and information retrieval. The tutorial promises to provide materials including presentation slides, a video teaser, and annotated references.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of this paper is relatively low. While the integration of LLMs into CIS is a hot topic, this work is presented as a *tutorial outline* rather than a novel research contribution. Many of the techniques mentioned (query expansion, query rewriting, user simulation) are well-established in the IR literature, even if applied in the context of LLMs. The value proposition is primarily in organizing and presenting this information in a coherent manner for a specific audience. The degree to which it presents new insights or combinations of techniques will depend on the actual tutorial content which is not available here.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate. CIS and the use of LLMs are rapidly evolving fields. A well-structured tutorial *could* be valuable in disseminating knowledge and guiding future research. However, the significance hinges on the quality of the tutorial itself, which is currently unknown. If it provides a comprehensive overview, critical analysis, and insightful synthesis of existing work, it could have a positive impact. The tutorial could help researchers and practitioners understand the current state of the art and identify promising avenues for future research. The inclusion of material such as video teasers and annotated references will aid in the widespread acceptance and understanding of these techniques.
    If the tutorial merely presents a superficial overview of existing techniques, the significance would be limited.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a relevant and timely topic.
    *   Covers a broad range of aspects related to query understanding in LLM-based CIS.
    *   Aims to provide practical resources (slides, video, annotated references).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   It's just an outline; the actual content and depth of the tutorial are unknown.
    *   Lack of demonstrated novelty in proposed methods.
    *   Depends heavily on the quality of the final tutorial.

*   **Potential Influence:** The potential influence is moderate. If the tutorial is well-received and provides valuable insights, it could help to accelerate research and development in the field. However, it's unlikely to have a transformative impact given the rapidly evolving nature of the area and the lack of groundbreaking innovation.

*   **Rigorous Rationale**

The provided paper is more of a roadmap than a fully-formed study. There is no new methodology introduced, no experiments are conducted or results presented, and no deep analysis of a particular novel aspect of the problem. Its value lies in its potential to educate and summarize, but without the tutorial content available it is difficult to ascertain its value. The paper mentions various aspects of query understanding, such as LLM evaluation, interactions, proactive query management, and enhancement. While these techniques are individually useful for researchers, the integration of these aspects to create better query understanding is unexplored in this paper. If there were more focus on exploring how these techniques interact, then it would warrant a higher score.

### Unifying Autoregressive and Diffusion-Based Sequence Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.06416v1)
- **Authors**: Nima Fathi, Torsten Scholak, Pierre-André Noël
- **Abstract**: We present significant extensions to diffusion-based sequence generation models, blurring the line with autoregressive language models. We introduce hyperschedules, which assign distinct noise schedules to individual token positions, generalizing both autoregressive models (e.g., GPT) and conventional diffusion models (e.g., SEDD, MDLM) as special cases. Second, we propose two hybrid token-wise noising processes that interpolate between absorbing and uniform processes, enabling the model to fix past mistakes, and we introduce a novel inference algorithm that leverages this new feature in a simplified context inspired from MDLM. To support efficient training and inference, we design attention masks compatible with KV-caching. Our methods achieve state-of-the-art perplexity and generate diverse, high-quality sequences across standard benchmarks, suggesting a promising path for autoregressive diffusion-based sequence generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces significant advancements in diffusion-based sequence generation models, aiming to bridge the gap with autoregressive language models.  It proposes several key innovations:

1.  **Hyperschedules:** This assigns different noise schedules to different token positions, generalizing both autoregressive (like GPT) and conventional diffusion models (like SEDD/MDLM) as special cases. This allows for finer control over the noising process.
2.  **Hybrid Token-Wise Noising Processes:**  These processes interpolate between absorbing and uniform noising, allowing the model to correct past mistakes during generation, inspired by masked diffusion language modeling (MDLM). A novel inference algorithm, Adaptive Correction Sampler (ACS) leverages this feature.
3.  **Attention Masks for KV-Caching:** The paper designs attention masks specifically tailored for these models, allowing for efficient training and inference by enabling KV-caching, a common optimization in autoregressive models.

The authors demonstrate that their methods achieve state-of-the-art perplexity and generate diverse, high-quality sequences on standard benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Unification of AR and Diffusion:** The paper presents a compelling argument for unifying autoregressive and diffusion models under a single framework using hyperschedules. This is a potentially significant conceptual contribution that can lead to new model architectures.
    *   **Practical Innovations:**  The proposed hyperschedules, hybrid noising processes, and adapted inference algorithms are not just theoretical constructs, but are shown to be effective in practice, leading to improved performance.  The ACS sampler offers a notable improvement.
    *   **KV-Caching for Efficiency:** Addressing the computational bottleneck of diffusion models by enabling KV-caching is a crucial and practical contribution, making the approach more viable for large-scale sequence generation.  This significantly improves the efficiency.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the superiority of the proposed methods compared to existing diffusion-based approaches.  State-of-the-art results are achieved in the specific context of diffusion sequence generation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty Relative to Recent MDM Work:** While the paper aims for generality beyond MDMs, some of the techniques (like adaptive correction sampling) and their motivations (mistake correction, remasking) directly echo developments in concurrent MDM research.  The paper also acknowledges that concurrent works have tackled elements discussed in the paper. While it generalizes some MDM findings to a non-MDM setting, the degree of novelty could be debated.
    *   **Complexity:** The approach introduces several hyperparameters and design choices (e.g., hyperschedule shape, noise interpolation) that require careful tuning, potentially increasing the difficulty of using the models. While efficient for a certain task it adds complexity.
    *   **Comparison with State-of-the-Art AR Models:**  While the paper compares to autoregressive *diffusion* models, it doesn't directly compare against the *best* autoregressive language models (e.g., extremely large transformers). The results are strong in the *diffusion sequence generation context*, but it's unclear if the approach can truly match the performance of highly optimized, scaled-up autoregressive models for general language modeling. It demonstrates a possible path for auto regressive diffusion sequence generation, but may not be comparable yet to autoregressive models.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper offers a novel perspective on sequence generation by unifying autoregressive and diffusion paradigms.
    *   The practical innovations (hyperschedules, hybrid noising, KV-caching) can accelerate research in diffusion-based language modeling.
    *   The empirical results suggest that diffusion models can be competitive with autoregressive models in sequence generation.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field. The unification of AR and diffusion models is a novel concept and the practical innovations lead to demonstrable performance gains. However, the novelty is somewhat tempered by the concurrent work in MDMs, and the comparison with the *best* autoregressive models is indirect. The complexity of tuning the additional hyperparameters is another factor. The paper is well-written and the experimental results support the claims. The potential influence on the field in the long run seems considerable as a starting point to bridge diffusion and AR models.

### Releasing Differentially Private Event Logs Using Generative Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.06418v1)
- **Authors**: Frederik Wangelik, Majid Rafiei, Mahsa Pourbafrani, Wil M. P. van der Aalst
- **Abstract**: In recent years, the industry has been witnessing an extended usage of process mining and automated event data analysis. Consequently, there is a rising significance in addressing privacy apprehensions related to the inclusion of sensitive and private information within event data utilized by process mining algorithms. State-of-the-art research mainly focuses on providing quantifiable privacy guarantees, e.g., via differential privacy, for trace variants that are used by the main process mining techniques, e.g., process discovery. However, privacy preservation techniques designed for the release of trace variants are still insufficient to meet all the demands of industry-scale utilization. Moreover, ensuring privacy guarantees in situations characterized by a high occurrence of infrequent trace variants remains a challenging endeavor. In this paper, we introduce two novel approaches for releasing differentially private trace variants based on trained generative models. With TraVaG, we leverage \textit{Generative Adversarial Networks} (GANs) to sample from a privatized implicit variant distribution. Our second method employs \textit{Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models} that reconstruct artificial trace variants from noise via trained Markov chains. Both methods offer industry-scale benefits and elevate the degree of privacy assurances, particularly in scenarios featuring a substantial prevalence of infrequent variants. Also, they overcome the shortcomings of conventional privacy preservation techniques, such as bounding the length of variants and introducing fake variants. Experimental results on real-life event data demonstrate that our approaches surpass state-of-the-art techniques in terms of privacy guarantees and utility preservation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of releasing differentially private (DP) event logs for process mining, focusing on scenarios with infrequent trace variants. It introduces two novel approaches: TraVaG, leveraging Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) to sample from a privatized implicit variant distribution, and a method based on Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPMs) to reconstruct artificial trace variants from noise. Both methods aim to overcome limitations of traditional DP techniques like prefix-based approaches, which introduce fake variants, remove true frequent variants, and have limited variant length.  The paper presents experimental results on real-life event data, demonstrating that the proposed approaches surpass state-of-the-art techniques in privacy guarantees and utility preservation. The study also offers a comparative analysis of the strengths and weaknesses of GAN-based and DDPM-based approaches in this context, discussing their suitability for different event log characteristics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates moderate novelty.  The primary innovation lies in applying generative models (GANs and DDPMs) in a differentially private setting specifically for event log release in process mining. While DP and generative models are not individually new, their combination in this context, particularly the application of DP-DDPM for privacy-preserving event log synthesis, represents a valuable contribution. The specific adaptations and comparisons between the GAN and DDPM architectures tailored for event log characteristics contribute to the incremental but important advances in the field.

*   **Significance:** The paper holds significant potential for impact within process mining and privacy-preserving data analysis. The ability to release useful event logs while maintaining strong privacy guarantees is crucial for wider adoption of process mining in sensitive domains like healthcare and finance. The paper's focus on addressing the challenges posed by infrequent trace variants is particularly relevant as these scenarios are common in real-world applications.  The comparison between the GAN and DDPM approaches provides practical guidance for choosing the most appropriate method based on data characteristics and privacy requirements. The study is well executed and provides sound empirical support for the developed framework using several real life event logs.
    However, the practical use of generative models relies on some key assumptions being met. That the statistics do not change dramatically over time and that training data is sufficient to model the data in a way which is statistically similar. These limitations are not a direct fault of the approach and are generally hard to directly resolve but a acknowledgement that some practical limitations do exist would be beneficial.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined methodologies leveraging state-of-the-art generative models.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation using real-life datasets.
    *   Detailed comparison of GAN and DDPM approaches.
    *   Addresses a practical and important challenge in privacy-preserving process mining.
    *   Empirical evidence supports superiority over state-of-the-art techniques.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The complexity of GAN and DDPM training may be a barrier to adoption for practitioners. The paper could benefit from more guidance on hyperparameter tuning and practical implementation.
    *   While the paper addresses some limitations of the proposed approaches, such as dependence on data characteristics, further discussion of the limitations and potential failure cases would strengthen the analysis.
    *   The empirical study, while thorough, could benefit from exploring the impact of the generative models' parameters (e.g. network size, number of epochs, epsilon/delta ranges) on the resulting trade-off between utility and privacy.
    * The statistical assumptions of the approach are largely untreated. Over what time periods is this stable?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in privacy-preserving process mining, particularly in exploring generative models for event log release.  It could also stimulate the development of more efficient and user-friendly tools and techniques for applying DP-GANs and DP-DDPMs in practice.

**Overall Score:** 7

**Justification:** The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by introducing and empirically evaluating two novel approaches for releasing differentially private event logs using generative models. The methods address a significant challenge (infrequent trace variants) and demonstrate superior performance compared to existing techniques. While the paper has some limitations regarding complexity and a further potential analysis of the statistical assumptions would be welcome. However, the paper exhibits sufficient novelty and potential for impact to warrant a score of 7.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### S'MoRE: Structural Mixture of Residual Experts for LLM Fine-tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.06426v1)
- **Authors**: Hanqing Zeng, Yinglong Xia, Zhuokai Zhao, Gilbert Jiang, Qiang Zhang, Jiayi Liu, Lizhu Zhang, Xiangjun Fan, Benyu Zhang
- **Abstract**: Fine-tuning pre-trained large language models (LLMs) presents a dual challenge of balancing parameter efficiency and model capacity. Existing methods like low-rank adaptations (LoRA) are efficient but lack flexibility, while Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures enhance model capacity at the cost of more & under-utilized parameters. To address these limitations, we propose Structural Mixture of Residual Experts (S'MoRE), a novel framework that seamlessly integrates the efficiency of LoRA with the flexibility of MoE. Specifically, S'MoRE employs hierarchical low-rank decomposition of expert weights, yielding residuals of varying orders interconnected in a multi-layer structure. By routing input tokens through sub-trees of residuals, S'MoRE emulates the capacity of many experts by instantiating and assembling just a few low-rank matrices. We craft the inter-layer propagation of S'MoRE's residuals as a special type of Graph Neural Network (GNN), and prove that under similar parameter budget, S'MoRE improves "structural flexibility" of traditional MoE (or Mixture-of-LoRA) by exponential order. Comprehensive theoretical analysis and empirical results demonstrate that S'MoRE achieves superior fine-tuning performance, offering a transformative approach for efficient LLM adaptation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "S'MORE: Structural Mixture of Residual Experts for LLM Fine-tuning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces S'MORE, a novel parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) technique for large language models (LLMs) that combines the efficiency of LoRA with the flexibility of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE). S'MORE uses a hierarchical low-rank decomposition of expert weights, creating a multi-layer structure where residuals of varying orders are interconnected. By routing input tokens through sub-trees of these residuals, S'MORE emulates the capacity of many experts using only a few low-rank matrices. The inter-layer propagation of residuals is treated as a special type of Graph Neural Network (GNN).  The authors theoretically prove that S'MORE improves the "structural flexibility" of traditional MoE by an exponential order with a similar parameter budget. The paper presents comprehensive theoretical analysis and empirical results demonstrating that S'MORE achieves superior fine-tuning performance compared to LoRA and MoE baselines, offering an efficient approach for LLM adaptation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of S'MORE – structurally arranging residual experts – is novel and addresses a key limitation of existing PEFT methods.  While MoE and LoRA are established techniques, combining them in a multi-layered, hierarchical architecture with GNN-like inter-layer residual propagation is a significant departure from prior work. The notion of "structural flexibility" and its theoretical quantification offer a new perspective on understanding the capacity of MoE-based systems.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles the critical challenge of balancing parameter efficiency and model capacity in LLM fine-tuning. Existing methods often excel in one aspect but fall short in the other. S'MORE aims to provide a better trade-off, which is highly relevant given the increasing scale of LLMs and the resource constraints associated with their adaptation. The demonstrated empirical improvements are significant, suggesting that S'MORE could become a valuable tool for efficient LLM fine-tuning.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Sound Theoretical Foundation:** The paper includes a strong theoretical analysis, including the proof of exponential improvement in "structural flexibility" and detailed parameter/computation cost analysis. These theoretical arguments provide a solid foundation for the empirical findings.
    *   **Comprehensive Experimental Evaluation:** The evaluation is thorough, covering different base models, fine-tuning benchmarks, router gate architectures, and scales. The consistent improvements across diverse settings strengthen the claim that S'MORE is a generally effective approach.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, making it relatively easy to understand the motivation, technical details, and experimental results.
    *   **Addresses a Key Problem:**  Parameter-efficient fine-tuning is crucial for the practical application of large language models, and this paper directly addresses that problem with a novel solution.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:**  S'MORE introduces significant architectural complexity, particularly with the hierarchical routing mechanism.  This may make it more difficult to implement and optimize compared to simpler methods like LoRA.
    *   **Limited Scale:** The authors acknowledge limitations in scaling the number of layers due to resource constraints. While the scaling-up experiments show promise, further investigation into how S'MORE performs with more layers and at larger scales is necessary.
    *   **Ablation of inter-layer propagation.**: While the paper is excellent at ablation studies across benchmarks it could have significantly benefited from ablation studies on what aspects of the inter-layer propagation of residuals contributes to performance gain.
    *   **Router Optimization:** The paper does not focus deeply on router optimization, which could be a crucial factor for the performance and scalability of S'MORE. It would be beneficial to explore more advanced routing techniques and analyze their impact.
    *   **Reliance on specific hardware and software:** The experimentation is limited to single node A100 GPU. It is unclear what the distributed training implications are, which may impact the overall result.

*   **Potential Influence:** If S'MORE's efficiency and effectiveness hold up at larger scales and with further optimization, it has the potential to significantly impact the field of LLM fine-tuning. It could lead to the development of more powerful and accessible LLMs by enabling efficient adaptation to diverse tasks and domains.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a novel and well-supported PEFT technique that addresses a crucial problem in LLM research. The theoretical analysis and comprehensive experimental evaluation provide strong evidence for its effectiveness. While the method has some limitations in terms of complexity and router optimization, its potential to improve the efficiency and accessibility of LLM fine-tuning makes it a valuable contribution.

### D-Feat Occlusions: Diffusion Features for Robustness to Partial Visual Occlusions in Object Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.06432v1)
- **Authors**: Rupayan Mallick, Sibo Dong, Nataniel Ruiz, Sarah Adel Bargal
- **Abstract**: Applications of diffusion models for visual tasks have been quite noteworthy. This paper targets making classification models more robust to occlusions for the task of object recognition by proposing a pipeline that utilizes a frozen diffusion model. Diffusion features have demonstrated success in image generation and image completion while understanding image context. Occlusion can be posed as an image completion problem by deeming the pixels of the occluder to be `missing.' We hypothesize that such features can help hallucinate object visual features behind occluding objects, and hence we propose using them to enable models to become more occlusion robust. We design experiments to include input-based augmentations as well as feature-based augmentations. Input-based augmentations involve finetuning on images where the occluder pixels are inpainted, and feature-based augmentations involve augmenting classification features with intermediate diffusion features. We demonstrate that our proposed use of diffusion-based features results in models that are more robust to partial object occlusions for both Transformers and ConvNets on ImageNet with simulated occlusions. We also propose a dataset that encompasses real-world occlusions and demonstrate that our method is more robust to partial object occlusions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "D-Feat Occlusions: Diffusion Features for Robustness to Partial Visual Occlusions in Object Recognition" investigates how diffusion models can improve the robustness of object recognition models (Transformers and ConvNets) to partial occlusions.  The authors propose two augmentation techniques: 1) input space augmentation, where occluded parts of an image are inpainted using a diffusion model, and 2) feature space augmentation, where intermediate features from a frozen diffusion model are combined with the primary classification features.  The method is evaluated on ImageNet with simulated occlusions and a newly created real-world occlusion dataset (D-Feat). The results suggest that both proposed techniques improve robustness to occlusions, with the feature space augmentation showing particularly strong performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of diffusion models to address occlusion robustness in object recognition is moderately novel. While diffusion models have been used for image completion/inpainting before, leveraging their intermediate features for augmentation in this specific context is a valuable contribution. The D-Feat dataset adds to the resources available for studying this problem with realistic occlusions.
*   **Significance:** The problem of occlusion robustness is highly relevant for real-world computer vision applications (autonomous driving, robotics, surveillance, medical imaging). The demonstrated performance gains from the proposed techniques suggest that diffusion models can offer a practical pathway to improving the reliability of object recognition systems in challenging environments. The creation of a real-world occlusion dataset is valuable for benchmarking and will enable the development of more realistic and robust models.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-motivated approach using diffusion models for hallucinating occluded image regions.
    *   Clear experimental design with ablation studies comparing different augmentation strategies.
    *   Evaluation on both simulated and real-world occlusion datasets.
    *   Demonstrates applicability across different architectures (Transformers and ConvNets).
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The simulated occlusions are relatively simple (black boxes). More complex and realistic occlusion patterns could be investigated in future work.
    *   While D-Feat is a useful contribution, the dataset has a relatively small size (300 images). A larger real-world occlusion dataset could improve the study's generalizability.
    *   The performance gains on ImageNet with simulated occlusions, although positive, are not dramatically large compared to some existing methods like CutMix.  It would be beneficial to compare against more state-of-the-art occlusion robustness methods.
    *   The reliance on a pre-trained Stable Diffusion model introduces a dependence on that model's capabilities and biases. Exploring other diffusion models or fine-tuning the diffusion model specifically for this task could be beneficial.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in occlusion robustness and the application of diffusion models for related tasks like domain adaptation or few-shot learning in occluded scenarios. The D-Feat dataset could become a useful benchmark for future research in the area.
*   **Critical Commentary:** The main strength lies in its practical application of diffusion models to a relevant computer vision problem. However, the performance gains, while positive, are not exceptionally significant, especially on simulated occlusions. The novelty lies in *how* the diffusion models are used, rather than a groundbreaking architectural contribution. The inclusion of the D-Feat dataset is positive, but its limited size is a limitation. The experimental section clearly shows a detailed comparison between different methods.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:** The paper provides a worthwhile, incremental contribution to the field of occlusion robustness by exploring the application of diffusion models. The approach is reasonably novel, and the D-Feat dataset is valuable. However, the limited size of the dataset and the modest performance gains, especially on simulated occlusions, constrain the overall significance of the work. The score reflects this balance between a sound technical approach and a notable contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Human Trust in AI Search: A Large-Scale Experiment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.06435v1)
- **Authors**: Haiwen Li, Sinan Aral
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly power generative search engines which, in turn, drive human information seeking and decision making at scale. The extent to which humans trust generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) can therefore influence what we buy, how we vote and our health. Unfortunately, no work establishes the causal effect of generative search designs on human trust. Here we execute ~12,000 search queries across seven countries, generating ~80,000 real-time GenAI and traditional search results, to understand the extent of current global exposure to GenAI search. We then use a preregistered, randomized experiment on a large study sample representative of the U.S. population to show that while participants trust GenAI search less than traditional search on average, reference links and citations significantly increase trust in GenAI, even when those links and citations are incorrect or hallucinated. Uncertainty highlighting, which reveals GenAI's confidence in its own conclusions, makes us less willing to trust and share generative information whether that confidence is high or low. Positive social feedback increases trust in GenAI while negative feedback reduces trust. These results imply that GenAI designs can increase trust in inaccurate and hallucinated information and reduce trust when GenAI's certainty is made explicit. Trust in GenAI varies by topic and with users' demographics, education, industry employment and GenAI experience, revealing which sub-populations are most vulnerable to GenAI misrepresentations. Trust, in turn, predicts behavior, as those who trust GenAI more click more and spend less time evaluating GenAI search results. These findings suggest directions for GenAI design to safely and productively address the AI "trust gap."
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates human trust in AI-powered search engines (GenAI) through a large-scale experiment. It examines the impact of GenAI design elements like reference links, uncertainty highlighting, explanations, and social feedback on user trust. The findings indicate that while GenAI is generally trusted less than traditional search, reference links and citations significantly increase trust even if incorrect. Uncertainty highlighting, regardless of confidence level, reduces trust. Positive social feedback increases trust, while negative feedback reduces it. The paper also analyzes how trust varies by demographics, topic, and query style, and the impact of trust on user behavior (clicks and time spent).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Scale and Rigor:** The study is a large-scale experiment with a representative U.S. sample, providing substantial statistical power. The pre-registered design and use of randomization inference are strong methodological choices.
    *   **Relevance:** The topic is highly relevant given the increasing prominence of GenAI in information retrieval and decision-making. Understanding trust in these systems is crucial.
    *   **Novelty (Limited):** While prior work has assessed the *quality* of GenAI's search results and the *use* of GenAI, the focus on *causal effects of design elements* on human *trust* is a valuable, although incremental, contribution.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings offer actionable insights for designers of GenAI search engines, particularly regarding the use of references and uncertainty indicators.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The examination of heterogeneity in trust across demographics and search topics provides a nuanced understanding of the issue.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generalizability:**  The exclusive focus on the U.S. market is a significant limitation. Cultural and linguistic factors likely influence trust perceptions. While the authors acknowledge this, the implications for global GenAI design are unclear.
    *   **Experiment Setting:** As the authors acknowledge, lab experiments, even when carefully designed to mimic real-world settings, might not fully capture the complexities of actual search behavior. The study does acknowledge limitations concerning search engines directly running similar studies "in the wild."
    *   **Hallucinated References:**  While the study notes that hallucinated references increased trust which may highlight something important in user bias, there are limits to knowing how much participants *perceived* the manipulated reference as invalid vs. just not noticing. The authors could have asked follow-up questions on this in the surveys.
    *   **Incremental Contribution:** While solid and well-executed, the work builds on prior research assessing the reliability and credibility of generative search information. The novelty is more in the causal manipulation of design elements than in identifying a completely new phenomenon.
    *   **Explanations Treatment:** Lack of Impact. The authors do not include what kind of explanations or models were added as part of the study. It is also potentially confounded by the fact that it used modified statements on what the models provide and it's pros and cons.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's core contribution lies in establishing the *causal* impact of specific GenAI design elements on user trust. While previous work has explored trust factors in web-based information and AI, the specific experimental manipulation within the context of generative search is a solid increment. However, the limitations concerning setting and generalizability of the finding to non-US regions, and the fact that results may be highly reliant on the model version at the time, do reduce its overall impact.

The paper contributes to the design of GenAI interfaces by shedding light on how seemingly small changes (e.g., adding references, even if flawed) can significantly influence user trust. The insights regarding uncertainty highlighting are particularly important.  That said, it also raises ethical concerns about the potential for AI designs to manipulate trust, even in the presence of inaccurate information.

**Score: 6.5/10**

**Justification:**

The paper's strengths are its methodological rigor, scale, and relevance.  However, its limitations—particularly the lack of generalizability beyond the U.S., experiment setting and concerns over incremental contributions and potential design obsolescence due to rapid evolution of GenAI systems lead to scoring between 6 and 7. As it helps the field, it is a solid "6," but some factors of the experimental design as well as the limited focus on other cultural or global contexts reduces the overall impact of the paper.

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 6/10

### Language-Dependent Political Bias in AI: A Study of ChatGPT and Gemini
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.06436v1)
- **Authors**: Dogus Yuksel, Mehmet Cem Catalbas, Bora Oc
- **Abstract**: As leading examples of large language models, ChatGPT and Gemini claim to provide accurate and unbiased information, emphasizing their commitment to political neutrality and avoidance of personal bias. This research investigates the political tendency of large language models and the existence of differentiation according to the query language. For this purpose, ChatGPT and Gemini were subjected to a political axis test using 14 different languages. The findings of the study suggest that these large language models do exhibit political tendencies, with both models demonstrating liberal and leftist biases. A comparative analysis revealed that Gemini exhibited a more pronounced liberal and left-wing tendency compared to ChatGPT. The study also found that these political biases varied depending on the language used for inquiry. The study delves into the factors that constitute political tendencies and linguistic differentiation, exploring differences in the sources and scope of educational data, structural and grammatical features of languages, cultural and political contexts, and the model's response to linguistic features. From this standpoint, and an ethical perspective, it is proposed that artificial intelligence tools should refrain from asserting a lack of political tendencies and neutrality, instead striving for political neutrality and executing user queries by incorporating these tendencies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the presence of political biases in ChatGPT and Gemini, two prominent large language models (LLMs). It examines whether these biases vary depending on the language used to formulate queries. The study employs a political compass test, translated into 14 different languages, to assess the political orientation of the LLMs. The results indicate that both models exhibit political tendencies, generally leaning towards liberal and leftist viewpoints, with Gemini showing a more pronounced tendency than ChatGPT. Furthermore, the study reveals that these biases fluctuate based on the input language. The paper then delves into potential contributing factors, including variations in training data, linguistic structures, cultural contexts, and the LLMs' response to language features. The authors conclude that AI tools should acknowledge and manage their potential biases to ensure political neutrality and user trust.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its multilingual approach to assessing political biases in LLMs. While previous studies have explored biases in these models, few have systematically examined how language impacts these biases. The use of 14 different languages and the comparison between ChatGPT and Gemini add value to the existing body of literature. The investigation into potential underlying reasons for this language-dependent bias, especially relating to data scarcity for certain languages, structural variations and cultural context provide incremental value.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial concern regarding the ethical implications of LLMs. Political bias in AI can have far-reaching consequences, potentially influencing public opinion, perpetuating stereotypes, and even impacting democratic processes. By highlighting the language-dependent nature of these biases, the study underscores the importance of considering cultural and linguistic contexts in AI development and deployment. Furthermore, it provides a pragmatic call for AI to be managed to reflect its biases rather than stating it has none.

**Strengths:**

*   **Methodological Rigor:** The paper utilizes a well-established tool, the political compass test, and adapts it for multilingual analysis. The data collection process, involving multiple queries to account for stochasticity in Gemini's responses, is also appropriate. Statistical testing provides a basis for its findings.

*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured, clearly explaining its research questions, methodology, results, and conclusions. The inclusion of tables and figures aid understanding.

*   **Discussion of Limitations:** The authors acknowledge limitations related to the free versions of the LLMs and the alteration of one of the questions to make the models answer. This honesty strengthens the paper's credibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Generality of Political Compass Test:** While widely used, the political compass test is a simplification of complex political ideologies. Its application across diverse cultures might introduce limitations. The test itself may be perceived as having biases.

*   **Limited Language Coverage:** While 14 languages is a good start, it is a small sample size compared to the hundreds of languages existing. The languages selected may not be representative of the world's languages, or their respective cultures.

*   **Causation vs. Correlation:** The study identifies correlations between language and bias but falls short in establishing clear causal links. The proposed explanations, while plausible, remain largely speculative without deeper investigation.

*   **Limited Scope of Bias Investigation:** The study focuses primarily on a left/right bias. Other forms of bias, such as racial, gender, or religious biases, are not considered.

*   **Lack of Depth:** The underlying explanation for the differences in bias tendencies between the language, beyond the data they were trained on is not explored.

**Potential Influence:** The paper contributes to a growing body of research on AI ethics and bias. It could influence future research by:

*   Encouraging more nuanced investigations into the cultural and linguistic dimensions of AI bias.
*   Motivating the development of debiasing techniques that account for language-specific contexts.
*   Informing the design of AI governance frameworks that promote fairness and transparency across diverse populations.

**Conclusion:**

Overall, the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of AI ethics by highlighting the language-dependent nature of political biases in LLMs. However, its limited language coverage, speculative explanations, and restricted focus on a single type of bias prevent it from being considered a groundbreaking study. The paper would benefit from more rigorous causal analysis and a broader scope of bias investigation.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:**

The paper demonstrates acceptable novelty with its multilingual investigation and provides significant insights into a critical issue. The findings are valuable and contribute to the ongoing discussion on AI ethics. However, it is not without limitations. A 6 is thus appropriate, indicating a paper that adds incremental knowledge, is well executed, but does not represent a major breakthrough. A score lower than 6 would not reflect the important contribution of its findings, particularly to the ethical considerations of AI in different regions. A score greater than 6 would only be appropriate if the causalities were confirmed and the limitations removed.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Don't Let It Hallucinate: Premise Verification via Retrieval-Augmented Logical Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.06438v1)
- **Authors**: Yuehan Qin, Shawn Li, Yi Nian, Xinyan Velocity Yu, Yue Zhao, Xuezhe Ma
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown substantial capacity for generating fluent, contextually appropriate responses. However, they can produce hallucinated outputs, especially when a user query includes one or more false premises-claims that contradict established facts. Such premises can mislead LLMs into offering fabricated or misleading details. Existing approaches include pretraining, fine-tuning, and inference-time techniques that often rely on access to logits or address hallucinations after they occur. These methods tend to be computationally expensive, require extensive training data, or lack proactive mechanisms to prevent hallucination before generation, limiting their efficiency in real-time applications. We propose a retrieval-based framework that identifies and addresses false premises before generation. Our method first transforms a user's query into a logical representation, then applies retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to assess the validity of each premise using factual sources. Finally, we incorporate the verification results into the LLM's prompt to maintain factual consistency in the final output. Experiments show that this approach effectively reduces hallucinations, improves factual accuracy, and does not require access to model logits or large-scale fine-tuning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Don't Let It Hallucinate: Premise Verification via Retrieval-Augmented Logical Reasoning" proposes a novel framework to mitigate hallucinations in Large Language Models (LLMs) by proactively identifying and addressing false premises in user queries *before* the LLM generates a response.  The method involves three key steps: (1) converting the user's query into a logical form to highlight key entities and relations, (2) using retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to check the accuracy of these statements against a knowledge graph, and (3) incorporating verification results into the LLM's prompt to ensure factual consistency in the final output.  The authors demonstrate that their approach effectively reduces hallucinations, improves factual accuracy, and doesn't require access to model logits or extensive fine-tuning, making it potentially more efficient for real-time applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of proactively addressing false premises *before* response generation is a reasonable improvement over post-hoc mitigation strategies. Transforming the query into a logical form for improved retrieval and verification is also a valuable contribution. The combination of these two strategies is what generates the paper's novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a very relevant and pressing problem in the field of LLMs, namely, the tendency to hallucinate, especially when presented with false premises. Reducing hallucinations is crucial for deploying LLMs in real-world applications, particularly in sensitive domains like finance and healthcare. The authors also correctly identify issues such as the high computational cost of fine-tuning based strategies, or the lack of mechanisms to prevent hallucination before generation in other approaches. If the proposed method truly achieves significant mitigation without excessive overhead, it could have a tangible impact.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Proactive approach:**  Focusing on preventing hallucinations rather than just detecting and correcting them afterward is a significant strength.
    *   **RAG with logical forms:**  Using logical forms to guide the retrieval process is a sensible way to improve the accuracy and relevance of retrieved knowledge.
    *   **No model access required:** The fact that the method does not rely on accessing the LLM's logits or internal weights makes it more easily adaptable to different LLMs, including those that are not openly accessible.
    *   The paper contains a reasonable number of ablations and comparisons to other approaches for both false-premise detection and hallucination mitigation in LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset limitations:** The experimental setup is limited to questions sourced from only one KG (KoPL) and only focuses on a limited number of relations. This makes it difficult to assess the generalizability of the method across different types of knowledge and question structures.
    *   **LLM Dependency for Logical Forms:** The extraction of logical forms relies on another LLM (GPT-4), which introduces a point of failure and potential bias. The complexity and accuracy of the logical forms might be a bottleneck.
    *   **Limited Baselines:** The baseline methods chosen for hallucination mitigation, though representative, aren't necessarily state-of-the-art in that specific sub-area. Some other mitigation strategies could have been implemented.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** Although accuracy is a reasonable metric, a deeper analysis of the types of hallucinations that are mitigated (and those that are not) would strengthen the paper. A comparison of generation speeds is not reported but would be helpful to understand the overhead of the method.
    *   **Prompt Sensitivity:** The prompt design used in the method can have a strong impact on its effectiveness. More discussion on prompt engineering and potential strategies for improving prompt robustness would be helpful.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in the following ways:
    *   Encouraging more work on proactive hallucination mitigation strategies.
    *   Promoting the use of logical forms for improving knowledge retrieval in LLMs.
    *   Highlighting the importance of handling false premises in user queries.
    *   Highlighting the need for evaluation datasets with diverse knowledge domains

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7** to this paper.

*   The core idea and the proposed method are novel and address an important problem. The method tackles limitations of existing approaches.
*   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the method, and the ablations add depth.
*   However, the paper has some weaknesses regarding the diversity of the evaluation dataset, the reliance on GPT-4 for logical form extraction, and evaluation metrics.

Therefore, while the paper is a valuable contribution to the field, its limitations prevent it from achieving a higher score. The approach is promising, but further validation and refinement are needed to assess its true potential.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can you Finetune your Binoculars? Embedding Text Watermarks into the Weights of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.06446v1)
- **Authors**: Fay Elhassan, Niccolò Ajroldi, Antonio Orvieto, Jonas Geiping
- **Abstract**: The indistinguishability of AI-generated content from human text raises challenges in transparency and accountability. While several methods exist to watermark models behind APIs, embedding watermark strategies directly into model weights that are later reflected in the outputs of the model is challenging. In this study we propose a strategy to finetune a pair of low-rank adapters of a model, one serving as the text-generating model, and the other as the detector, so that a subtle watermark is embedded into the text generated by the first model and simultaneously optimized for detectability by the second. In this way, the watermarking strategy is fully learned end-to-end. This process imposes an optimization challenge, as balancing watermark robustness, naturalness, and task performance requires trade-offs. We discuss strategies on how to optimize this min-max objective and present results showing the effect of this modification to instruction finetuning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel end-to-end framework for embedding text watermarks directly into the weights of Large Language Models (LLMs).  It uses a min-max optimization approach where two LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) adapters are trained simultaneously: one acts as the text generator ("performer"), and the other as a watermark detector ("observer"). The training is guided by a modified "Binoculars" objective which aims to maximize the difference in perplexity between the two models when evaluating machine-generated vs. human-written text. The authors address the instability of min-max training by reformulating the problem as a constrained optimization, controlling the loss function to prevent excessive optimization. The effectiveness of the approach is demonstrated through experiments on instruction fine-tuning tasks, evaluating watermark detectability, text fluency, and classification accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the *end-to-end* training strategy to directly embed watermarks within LLM weights using a *pair* of models, a generator and detector, optimized simultaneously.  While previous works have explored watermarking techniques, many focus on inference-time modifications or post-hoc embedding.  The explicit use of a trained detector as part of the training loop is a meaningful contribution. The use of the Binoculars metric is a good starting point, but the modification and application in this end-to-end framework are novel.

* **Significance:**  The paper tackles a significant challenge in the field of AI accountability: ensuring the traceability of AI-generated content when deployed using open-source models.  Embedding watermarks directly into model weights provides a more robust and potentially less easily circumvented mechanism compared to methods that rely on altering sampling procedures or post-hoc modifications. The work has practical relevance to addressing issues of misinformation, content moderation, and intellectual property. However, this is an initial step and the demonstrated gains are relatively modest. Also, this approach is not totally circumventable. An attacker could continue to fine-tune the watermarked model with a larger dataset in order to "wash out" the subtle differences between the "observer" and "performer" models.

* **Strengths:**
    * The paper proposes a well-defined and technically sound approach.
    * The use of LoRA adapters allows for efficient training and reduces the computational burden.
    * The constrained optimization helps to stabilize training and balance watermark detectability with text fluency.
    * The experimental results, while not groundbreaking, provide evidence that the method works and demonstrate the impact of different regularization strategies and LoRA configurations.
    * The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and experimental setup.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The improvements in performance metrics (ROC-AUC, PR-AUC) are relatively small, suggesting that the watermark is subtle, but potentially also less robust.
    * The paper lacks a thorough evaluation of the watermark's robustness against adversarial attacks or paraphrasing techniques designed to remove or weaken the watermark.
    * The use of a modified Binoculars metric, while sensible, raises the question of whether the detector is simply memorizing specific generation characteristics of the performer, rather than truly detecting a generalizable watermark signal.
    * The overall effect of the modifications on fluency and text coherence is limited. This is a good aspect of the model, but also limits its real-world application since it requires more research on balancing robustness and detectability with fluency.
    * The work has not been scaled to very large models or more complex watermarking schemes. The results are limited to a specific LLM architecture.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and reliable watermarking techniques for LLMs.  It can serve as a foundation for future research exploring different training objectives, optimization strategies, and defense mechanisms against watermark removal attacks. The work also highlights the importance of balancing watermark detectability with text quality and the need for careful regularization during training.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to a practically relevant problem, the modest performance gains and lack of robustness analysis limit its immediate impact. The idea of end-to-end training of watermark embedding and detection is sound, but the results require further validation and improvement. The constrained optimization strategy is a valuable contribution, but more exploration of different constraints and objectives is needed. Therefore, a score reflecting a good initial contribution, but with room for significant improvement, is warranted.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Can LLMs Simulate Personas with Reversed Performance? A Benchmark for Counterfactual Instruction Following
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.06460v1)
- **Authors**: Sai Adith Senthil Kumar, Hao Yan, Saipavan Perepa, Murong Yue, Ziyu Yao
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are now increasingly widely used to simulate personas in virtual environments, leveraging their instruction-following capability. However, we discovered that even state-of-the-art LLMs cannot simulate personas with reversed performance (e.g., student personas with low proficiency in educational settings), which impairs the simulation diversity and limits the practical applications of the simulated environments. In this work, using mathematical reasoning as a representative scenario, we propose the first benchmark dataset for evaluating LLMs on simulating personas with reversed performance, a capability that we dub "counterfactual instruction following". We evaluate both open-weight and closed-source LLMs on this task and find that LLMs, including the OpenAI o1 reasoning model, all struggle to follow counterfactual instructions for simulating reversedly performing personas. Intersectionally simulating both the performance level and the race population of a persona worsens the effect even further. These results highlight the challenges of counterfactual instruction following and the need for further research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Can LLMs Simulate Personas with Reversed Performance? A Benchmark for Counterfactual Instruction Following" investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to simulate personas with intentionally *reversed* or low performance. The authors argue that while LLMs excel at simulating high-performing personas, they struggle to accurately simulate personas that exhibit misunderstandings, errors, or incomplete reasoning, which limits the diversity and practical application of persona simulation in fields like education. They introduce REVERSO, a new benchmark dataset based on mathematical reasoning (adapted from GSM8k), to evaluate LLMs on this "counterfactual instruction following" capability. REVERSO includes simulating both high- and low-performing students, as well as an intersectional setting where the race of the simulated persona is also specified.  The study evaluates several open-weight and closed-source LLMs and finds that even advanced models struggle to reliably simulate personas with reversed performance, particularly in the intersectional setting. They propose a new metric, Degree of Contrast (DoC), to measure how well LLMs differentiate between high- and low-performing personas in their reasoning behavior. The results highlight the challenges of counterfactual instruction following for LLMs and the need for further research.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper addresses an important and relatively unexplored aspect of LLM-based persona simulation.  While existing research focuses on generating competent and accurate personas, the ability to simulate personas with realistic weaknesses and limitations is crucial for various applications, particularly in education and training scenarios.

Here's a breakdown of the paper's strengths and weaknesses:

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Problem Formulation:** The paper identifies a real limitation of current LLM persona simulation capabilities.  The concept of "counterfactual instruction following" provides a useful framework for understanding and addressing this limitation.
*   **Relevant Application Domain:** The choice of mathematics education as the application domain is compelling. Simulating students with varying levels of understanding is highly valuable for improving educational tools and techniques.
*   **Well-Designed Benchmark Dataset (REVERSO):**  The REVERSO dataset, adapted from GSM8k, provides a standardized and readily accessible resource for evaluating LLMs on the specific task of counterfactual instruction following. The integration of race adds a layer of complexity and addresses potential biases.
*   **Quantitative and Qualitative Evaluation:**  The paper uses both accuracy and the novel DoC metric to evaluate performance.  The inclusion of human evaluation for DoC validates the metric's effectiveness. The detailed analysis of error patterns and reasoning behaviors provides valuable insights.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The study examines a range of LLMs (both open-source and closed-source) and prompting strategies, providing a comprehensive overview of current capabilities and limitations.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written, clearly structured, and presents its findings in a concise and accessible manner.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Adaptation, not creation:** REVERSO is adapted from GSM8K dataset, the novelty of benchmark is somewhat reduced, since it doesn't bring new context to LLMs.
*   **Limited Scope of Counterfactual Behavior:** The paper focuses primarily on *low* performance in mathematical reasoning.  While valuable, the concept of "reversed performance" could be broadened to include other types of counterfactual behaviors, such as simulating personas with specific biases or emotional tendencies.
*   **Dependency on Instruction Following Quality:** The definition of DoC depends on the quality of instruction following, it may be hard to distinguish whether DoC result is due to instruction following issue rather than following counterfactual behavior.
*   **Lack of Theoretical Explanation:** While the paper demonstrates empirical limitations, it lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of *why* LLMs struggle with counterfactual instruction following.  Exploring the underlying mechanisms that prevent LLMs from simulating reversed performance could provide valuable insights for future research.
*   ** limited prompting strategy.** It only use 3 prompting strategy while there are other potentially more suitable approaches that are missing such as instruction tuning.
*   **Limited Solution Exploration:** The paper identifies the problem but doesn't propose or evaluate concrete solutions for improving LLMs' ability to follow counterfactual instructions. While this is understandable within the scope of a benchmark paper, it limits the overall impact.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by highlighting a previously overlooked limitation of LLM-based persona simulation. The findings have important implications for researchers and practitioners working in education, training, and other areas where realistic persona simulation is crucial.  The REVERSO dataset and DoC metric provide valuable tools for future research in this area.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper makes a solid contribution to the field, but could be improved by further development of prompt strategies and more theoretical exploration. The problem is well-defined, the benchmark is useful, and the evaluation is thorough. However, the limited exploration of solutions and the reliance on adaptation rather than creation prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution.

### Mind the Gap: Evaluating Vision Systems in Small Data Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.06486v1)
- **Authors**: Samuel Stevens, S M Rayeed, Jenna Kline
- **Abstract**: The practical application of AI tools for specific computer vision tasks relies on the "small-data regime" of hundreds to thousands of labeled samples. This small-data regime is vital for applications requiring expensive expert annotations, such as ecological monitoring, medical diagnostics or industrial quality control. We find, however, that computer vision research has ignored the small data regime as evaluations increasingly focus on zero- and few-shot learning. We use the Natural World Tasks (NeWT) benchmark to compare multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) and vision-only methods across varying training set sizes. MLLMs exhibit early performance plateaus, while vision-only methods improve throughout the small-data regime, with performance gaps widening beyond 10 training examples. We provide the first comprehensive comparison between these approaches in small-data contexts and advocate for explicit small-data evaluations in AI research to better bridge theoretical advances with practical deployments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Mind the Gap: Evaluating Vision Systems in Small Data Applications" addresses the under-evaluation of vision systems in the "small-data regime" (hundreds to thousands of labeled samples), a scenario critical for numerous real-world applications like ecological monitoring, medical diagnostics, and industrial quality control.  The authors argue that the AI research community has increasingly focused on zero-shot and few-shot learning, neglecting the practical importance of small-data applications. They use the Natural World Tasks (NeWT) benchmark to compare the performance of multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) and vision-only methods across varying training set sizes in ecological classification tasks.  The study reveals that MLLMs exhibit early performance plateaus, while vision-only methods demonstrate consistent improvement throughout the small-data regime.  The authors advocate for explicit small-data evaluations in AI research to better align theoretical advancements with practical deployments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic comparison of MLLMs and vision-only methods within the small-data regime. While the individual models are not new, the direct comparison and the highlighting of distinct performance scaling characteristics are novel. The observation of early performance plateaus in MLLMs when compared to vision-only systems as data availability increases in a practical setting provides new insights into the utility of both approaches.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial gap in the current AI research landscape. The prevailing focus on zero-shot and few-shot learning risks creating a disconnect between theoretical progress and practical needs. Emphasizing the importance of small-data evaluations and providing empirical evidence of performance differences is significant.  It encourages a more balanced approach to benchmarking and model development. While ecologically-motivated binary classification tasks are selected as the primary test setting, the findings could be applicable to other similar image classification tasks that face data availability constraints.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the "small-data gap" and its practical implications.
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The experimental methodology is well-defined, with controlled variations in training set sizes and standardized prompting/parsing procedures.
    *   **Empirical Evidence:** The paper provides substantial empirical evidence to support its claims, using a relevant benchmark (NeWT) and comparing various state-of-the-art models.
    *   **Actionable Recommendations:**  The authors explicitly advocate for the inclusion of small-data evaluations in AI research benchmarks, offering a clear call to action.
    *   Availability of code and results for reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Task Specificity:**  The results are primarily based on ecological classification tasks. Although ecological tasks serve as a good representative of common characteristics in real-world constrained classification scenarios, more broad applicability to other computer vision areas would strengthen its conclusions.
    *   **Limited Model Variety:** While the paper compares MLLMs and vision-only systems, there is room for considering methods specifically designed for small-data learning (e.g., meta-learning, active learning) to present a more comprehensive view.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   Raising awareness about the importance of small-data evaluations.
    *   Encouraging the development of benchmarks and evaluation protocols that specifically target small-data scenarios.
    *   Guiding model selection and development decisions in practical applications where data availability is limited.

*   **Justification:** The paper offers a valuable contribution by systematically investigating a regime that has received less attention in comparison to the current trends in zero-shot and few-shot learning. It provides a nuanced understanding of MLLM and vision-only method performance characteristics and advocates for better alignment between research and application. The limitations of focusing solely on the ecologically-motivated NeWT benchmark is appropriately acknowledged, though this aspect limits broader impacts of the findings. In combination with a relatively limited depth of methodological novelty in small-data learning techniques, this limits the degree of the contribution.
