### DeduCE: Deductive Consistency as a Framework to Evaluate LLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07080v1)
- **Authors**: Atharva Pandey, Kshitij Dubey, Rahul Sharma, Amit Sharma
- **Abstract**: Despite great performance on Olympiad-level reasoning problems, frontier large language models can still struggle on high school math when presented with novel problems outside standard benchmarks. Going beyond final accuracy, we propose a deductive consistency metric to analyze chain-of-thought output from language models (LMs).Formally, deductive reasoning involves two subtasks: understanding a set of input premises and inferring the conclusions that follow from them. The proposed metric studies LMs' performance on these subtasks, with the goal of explaining LMs' reasoning errors on novel problems: how well do LMs understand input premises with increasing context lengths, and how well can they infer conclusions over multiple reasoning hops? Since existing benchmarks may be memorized, we develop a pipeline to evaluate LMs' deductive consistency on novel, perturbed versions of benchmark problems. On novel grade school math problems (GSM-8k), we find that LMs are fairly robust to increasing number of input premises, but suffer significant accuracy decay as the number of reasoning hops is increased. Interestingly, these errors are masked in the original benchmark as all models achieve near 100% accuracy. As we increase the number of solution steps using a synthetic dataset, prediction over multiple hops still remains the major source of error compared to understanding input premises. Other factors, such as shifts in language style or natural propagation of early errors do not explain the trends. Our analysis provides a new view to characterize LM reasoning -- as computations over a window of input premises and reasoning hops -- that can provide unified evaluation across problem domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel metric called "deductive consistency" to analyze the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) in a more fine-grained manner than simply evaluating final accuracy. The approach involves assessing how well LLMs understand input premises and how consistently they infer conclusions over multiple reasoning steps.  The authors develop a pipeline ("DeduCE") to evaluate deductive consistency on novel, perturbed versions of existing benchmark problems, specifically GSM8K. They templatize problems, generate code solutions, mutate problems by changing variables, and then use LLMs to solve them, comparing the LLM's reasoning steps to the correct derivations. The results show that while LLMs can be robust to increasing input premise length, they struggle as the number of reasoning hops increases. They also find evidence suggesting that memorization masks reasoning errors on original benchmark problems and that fine-tuning may improve task-specific performance but not necessarily general deductive reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The concept of "deductive consistency" is a valuable contribution.  While the idea of analyzing intermediate steps in reasoning is not entirely new (e.g., work on explanation evaluation), this paper formalizes it into a specific metric and applies it within a pipeline designed to avoid memorization effects.  The use of code generation to create a "ground truth" for intermediate steps, albeit imperfect, is also a clever approach. The introduction of varying "hops" and "prefixes" to granularly analyze reasoning breakdowns is a significant contribution.
* **Significance:** The paper is significant because it highlights a crucial limitation of current LLMs: their ability to maintain consistent reasoning across longer chains of inference, even when they perform well on standard benchmarks.  The findings suggest that improvements in final accuracy might mask underlying reasoning deficiencies. The decomposition of the problem into understanding premises and performing inferences across hops allows for a more targeted analysis of LLM limitations. Understanding what goes wrong where is a big deal for guiding further improvements on the LLMs.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear and well-defined metric:** Deductive consistency is clearly defined and parameterized.
    * **Rigorous methodology:** The DeduCE pipeline is well-described and attempts to address the crucial issue of memorization.
    * **Interesting findings:** The observation that LLMs are more susceptible to errors with increasing hops than with increasing premise length is insightful.
    * **Attempts to rule out alternative explanations:** The authors explore and attempt to rule out alternative explanations for the observed accuracy drop (e.g., style changes, error propagation).
    * **Application to different dataset type:** Experiments on SynDeduct demonstrates the generality of the approach.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Code Generation for Ground Truth:**  The pipeline's reliability hinges on the ability to generate *correct* code solutions from the original problems. This introduces a potential source of error if the code generation process is flawed or if the generated code is not truly representative of all possible solutions. It remains to be proven on how general this ground truth could be.
    * **Limited Scope of Perturbations:** Only variable changes are explored. Future work can experiment with adding distractor premises, and style changes, or other types of pertubation.
    * **Simplicity of GSM8K:** While using GSM8K provides a starting point, the simplicity of these problems limits the generalizability of the findings to more complex reasoning tasks. The hop number only accounts for the arithmatic operations without taking into account the reasoning part. The study uses number of examples for each value of "hops" rather than keeping it constant, which introduces statistical bias.
    * **Error analysis is somewhat shallow:** The error analysis relies on GPT-4 for categorization, which is also subject to limitations and biases. While the findings point to a high proportion of calculation errors, it doesn't delve deeply into *why* these errors occur.
    * **SynDeduct dataset generation:** Details of DAG construction are missing from the current draft.

**Potential Influence:**  This paper has the potential to influence the design of future LLM evaluation benchmarks, emphasizing the need for metrics that go beyond final accuracy. It could also inspire research into techniques for improving the consistency and reliability of LLM reasoning, perhaps by focusing on methods that enhance inferential capabilities over longer sequences of steps. The work can also inspire improvements to code generation for other structured reasoning evaluations.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:** The paper introduces a valuable metric and a well-designed evaluation framework for analyzing LLM reasoning. The findings are significant, demonstrating limitations that are often masked by current evaluation methods.  The main weaknesses lie in the dependence on code generation as ground truth, the restricted scope of perturbations explored, and the simplicity of the GSM8K dataset.  While not a revolutionary breakthrough, the work represents a solid and important contribution to the field of LLM evaluation, providing a more nuanced understanding of LLM reasoning capabilities. Therefore, a score of 7 is given.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### KG-LLM-Bench: A Scalable Benchmark for Evaluating LLM Reasoning on Textualized Knowledge Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07087v1)
- **Authors**: Elan Markowitz, Krupa Galiya, Greg Ver Steeg, Aram Galstyan
- **Abstract**: Knowledge graphs have emerged as a popular method for injecting up-to-date, factual knowledge into large language models (LLMs). This is typically achieved by converting the knowledge graph into text that the LLM can process in context. While multiple methods of encoding knowledge graphs have been proposed, the impact of this textualization process on LLM performance remains under-explored. We introduce KG-LLM-Bench, a comprehensive and extensible benchmark spanning five knowledge graph understanding tasks, and evaluate how different encoding strategies affect performance across various base models. Our extensive experiments with seven language models and five textualization strategies provide insights for optimizing LLM performance on KG reasoning tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KG-LLM-Bench, a new benchmark for evaluating the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) when processing knowledge graphs. It addresses the under-explored impact of *textualization strategies* – the ways knowledge graphs are converted into text for LLMs to ingest – on performance. The benchmark includes five knowledge graph understanding tasks. The authors conducted extensive experiments involving seven LLMs and five different textualization methods. Their findings reveal that the textualization strategy significantly impacts performance, demonstrating that choosing the right encoding can improve benchmark results. They also investigated the effect of pseudonymization to reduce reliance on pre-trained knowledge and examined token efficiency across textualization strategies. The benchmark is publicly available for future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in systematically evaluating the effect of various textualization strategies on LLMs' ability to reason over knowledge graphs. While individual components (KGQA, LLMs, different KG encodings) are not novel in themselves, the *combination* and thorough empirical study provide new insights. The pseudonymization analysis to minimize pre-trained knowledge influence is also a positive addition.

*   **Significance:** The findings have practical implications.  The paper highlights that simply dumping a KG into a list of triples may *not* be optimal and that performance can be boosted by carefully selecting (or even tailoring) the textualization method. The benchmark itself provides a standardized and extensible way to evaluate future KG+LLM approaches, potentially accelerating research in this area. The benchmark contributes a structured and scalable means to analyze LLM understanding of knowledge graphs, filling a notable gap in evaluation methodologies and promoting a deeper understanding of optimal KG representation techniques.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive empirical evaluation: The extensive experiments across multiple models, tasks, and textualization strategies provide substantial evidence for the main claims.
    *   Publicly available benchmark: The release of KG-LLM-Bench ensures reproducibility and encourages further research and extensions.
    *   Focus on a practical problem: The paper tackles a real-world concern of how to best integrate KGs with LLMs, making it highly relevant for applications.
    *   Clear problem definition and methodology: The methodology is clearly articulated, which makes the experimental setup easily understandable.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited Domain/Graph Type: The benchmark only uses one knowledge graph (Countries from WikiData). Results might not generalize well to other domains or graph types with different structural properties. While acknowledging this in the limitations, it would be beneficial to include experiments with more diverse knowledge graphs.
    *   Exact Match Evaluation: The reliance on exact match for evaluation is restrictive. It doesn't account for paraphrasing, synonyms, or other forms of semantically correct answers. This overly penalizes models. While simplicity is useful, other studies show that it can obfuscate results because of the complexity of LLMs and how they formulate answers.
    *   Lack of Theoretical Grounding: While the paper provides empirical results, it lacks a strong theoretical grounding to explain *why* certain textualization strategies work better than others for specific models and tasks. This makes it more difficult to generalize the findings.

*   **Potential Influence:** KG-LLM-Bench could become a standard evaluation tool in the KG+LLM research community, influencing the design of future methods.  The findings about textualization strategies should also prompt researchers to pay more attention to this aspect, rather than treating all encodings as equivalent.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper makes a *solid* contribution by highlighting the importance of textualization strategies. The benchmark, its comprehensive methodology, and significant findings justify a positive evaluation. However, limitations around graph diversity and evaluation metrics restrain it from receiving a higher rating.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### OmniCaptioner: One Captioner to Rule Them All
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07089v1)
- **Authors**: Yiting Lu, Jiakang Yuan, Zhen Li, Shitian Zhao, Qi Qin, Xinyue Li, Le Zhuo, Licheng Wen, Dongyang Liu, Yuewen Cao, Xiangchao Yan, Xin Li, Botian Shi, Tao Chen, Zhibo Chen, Lei Bai, Bo Zhang, Peng Gao
- **Abstract**: We propose OmniCaptioner, a versatile visual captioning framework for generating fine-grained textual descriptions across a wide variety of visual domains. Unlike prior methods limited to specific image types (e.g., natural images or geometric visuals), our framework provides a unified solution for captioning natural images, visual text (e.g., posters, UIs, textbooks), and structured visuals (e.g., documents, tables, charts). By converting low-level pixel information into semantically rich textual representations, our framework bridges the gap between visual and textual modalities. Our results highlight three key advantages: (i) Enhanced Visual Reasoning with LLMs, where long-context captions of visual modalities empower LLMs, particularly the DeepSeek-R1 series, to reason effectively in multimodal scenarios; (ii) Improved Image Generation, where detailed captions improve tasks like text-to-image generation and image transformation; and (iii) Efficient Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), which enables faster convergence with less data. We believe the versatility and adaptability of OmniCaptioner can offer a new perspective for bridging the gap between language and visual modalities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "OMNICAPTIONER: One Captioner to Rule Them All":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OMNICAPTIONER, a novel visual captioning framework designed to generate fine-grained textual descriptions across a diverse range of visual domains. Unlike existing methods that are often limited to specific image types (e.g., natural images or geometric visuals), OMNICAPTIONER offers a unified solution for captioning natural images, visual text (posters, UI, textbooks), and structured visuals (charts, tables, equations). The framework converts low-level pixel information into semantically rich textual representations, effectively bridging the gap between visual and textual modalities.  The authors showcase OMNICAPTIONER's effectiveness in enhancing visual reasoning with large language models (LLMs), improving image generation tasks, and facilitating efficient supervised fine-tuning (SFT). The core innovation involves a two-stage caption generation pipeline, including Seed-Caption Generation to ensure precise pixel-to-word mapping and Caption Extension to enrich caption styles. A large, diverse caption dataset of 21M image-caption pairs are collected and leveraged.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Domain Versatility:** The primary strength lies in its ability to handle a wide spectrum of visual domains in a unified framework.  This is a significant step forward, as prior art often focused on specific domains.
    *   **High-Quality Captions:** The paper highlights the generation of *fine-grained* and *detailed* captions. This level of detail is crucial for tasks like visual reasoning, image generation and few-shot adaptation, where nuanced understanding is key.
    *   **Improved Visual Reasoning:** Leveraging detailed captions for visual reasoning with LLMs is a compelling application, demonstrating the potential to offload visual processing to textual understanding. This could be significant given how computationally expensive vision-language alignment is, and how well LLMs can reason.
    *   **Efficient Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT):**  The experimental results suggest faster convergence and improved performance in SFT tasks, indicating the potential of OMNICAPTIONER as an effective pretraining strategy. This is a welcome addition as SFT adaptation is a vital part of any new pretraining stage.
    *   **Comprehensive Dataset:**  The curated dataset covering a wide range of visual modalities is a substantial resource and contributes to the framework's effectiveness.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Closed-Source Models:** The pipeline relies heavily on GPT-4o for seed caption generation. This reliance makes the framework less accessible to researchers without access to these proprietary models. Furthermore, any changes that occur within GPT4o will affect the pipeline's functionality, reducing replicability.
    *   **Limited Novelty in Captioning Pipeline:** While the idea of two stage caption generation is not necessarily groundbreaking, the sheer scale is noteworthy, and how the authors were able to demonstrate this performance with the large dataset is interesting.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** The evaluation relies on existing benchmarks that might not fully capture the nuances of diverse visual domains. More specialized metrics could better assess the quality of the generated captions.
    *   **Lack of Visualisation of SFT task** While visualising the improved visual reasoning with LLMs and Enhanced Image Generation, there is no visualisation to highlight how detailed the pixel to text mapping is.
    *   **High Cost of Initial Seed Caption generation:** Generating 21 million captions with GPT-4o is an expensive undertaking. This might be prohibitive for some researchers.
*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The key novelty lies in the unified captioning framework capable of handling a broad range of visual domains with high-quality, detailed descriptions. The conversion of pixel to text is an existing concept. However the ability to expand this mapping effectively across a broad range of visual inputs sets it apart from previous endeavors.
    *   The performance boost on visual reasoning tasks is significant, demonstrating the potential of detailed captions to improve LLMs' ability to process visual information.
    *   The improvements observed in image generation and efficient SFT further highlight the practical value of the framework.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of visual captioning and multimodal learning. OMNICAPTIONER's domain versatility, detailed captions, and positive impact on downstream tasks are noteworthy. However, the reliance on GPT-4o and the scope for improved evaluation metrics slightly temper the overall impact. Given these considerations, a score of 7.5/10 is appropriate. This reflects the paper's significant advancements while acknowledging its limitations and potential for future refinement. It is more towards the 7 side as it relies heavily on GPT4o which reduces reproducability.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Sculpting Subspaces: Constrained Full Fine-Tuning in LLMs for Continual Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07097v1)
- **Authors**: Nikhil Shivakumar Nayak, Krishnateja Killamsetty, Ligong Han, Abhishek Bhandwaldar, Prateek Chanda, Kai Xu, Hao Wang, Aldo Pareja, Oleg Silkin, Mustafa Eyceoz, Akash Srivastava
- **Abstract**: Continual learning in large language models (LLMs) is prone to catastrophic forgetting, where adapting to new tasks significantly degrades performance on previously learned ones. Existing methods typically rely on low-rank, parameter-efficient updates that limit the model's expressivity and introduce additional parameters per task, leading to scalability issues. To address these limitations, we propose a novel continual full fine-tuning approach leveraging adaptive singular value decomposition (SVD). Our method dynamically identifies task-specific low-rank parameter subspaces and constrains updates to be orthogonal to critical directions associated with prior tasks, thus effectively minimizing interference without additional parameter overhead or storing previous task gradients. We evaluate our approach extensively on standard continual learning benchmarks using both encoder-decoder (T5-Large) and decoder-only (LLaMA-2 7B) models, spanning diverse tasks including classification, generation, and reasoning. Empirically, our method achieves state-of-the-art results, up to 7% higher average accuracy than recent baselines like O-LoRA, and notably maintains the model's general linguistic capabilities, instruction-following accuracy, and safety throughout the continual learning process by reducing forgetting to near-negligible levels. Our adaptive SVD framework effectively balances model plasticity and knowledge retention, providing a practical, theoretically grounded, and computationally scalable solution for continual learning scenarios in large language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Sculpting Subspaces: Constrained Full Fine-Tuning in LLMs for Continual Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel continual learning approach for large language models (LLMs) called constrained full fine-tuning using adaptive singular value decomposition (SVD).  The method addresses catastrophic forgetting by dynamically identifying task-specific low-rank parameter subspaces and constraining updates to be orthogonal to critical directions associated with prior tasks. This enables full parameter updates without storing previous task gradients or introducing additional parameters. The approach is evaluated on standard continual learning benchmarks using both encoder-decoder (T5-Large) and decoder-only (LLaMA-2 7B) models and shows state-of-the-art performance, improved knowledge retention, and negligible forgetting. The adaptive SVD framework balances model plasticity and knowledge retention.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novel Approach:** The use of adaptive SVD for continual learning is a novel approach. Existing methods often rely on parameter-efficient fine-tuning, which limits model expressiveness, or introduce extra parameters.  Constraining updates via orthogonality to prior task subspaces offers a unique way to maintain plasticity while preserving knowledge.
    * **Strong Performance:** The experimental results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on several benchmarks, outperforming recent baselines like O-LoRA by a significant margin. The reported improvement in average accuracy (up to 7%) is substantial. The demonstration of preserving general linguistic capabilities, instruction-following, and safety is a notable strength.
    * **Scalability:** The fixed parameter count, regardless of task sequence length, addresses a key limitation of many existing continual learning methods that suffer from parameter growth. This makes the approach more scalable for real-world deployment.
    * **Theoretical Grounding:** The paper offers a theoretical justification for the approach, relating the adaptive rank selection to Hessian eigenvalues and curvature.  This provides a more robust foundation compared to purely empirical methods. The detailed appendix with theoretical analysis adds significant weight to the paper.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The use of diverse tasks (classification, generation, reasoning), different model architectures (T5-Large, LLaMA-2 7B), and standard benchmarks strengthens the validity of the results.  The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of each component of the method.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **SVD Computation Cost:** The paper acknowledges the computational overhead associated with repeated SVD computations. While the method avoids parameter growth, the cost of SVD could still be a bottleneck, especially for very large models and long task sequences.
    * **Rank Estimation Sensitivity:** The reported performance drop with inaccurate rank selection highlights a potential vulnerability. More robust and data-driven methods for determining effective rank are needed. The reliance on a hyperparameter (I(l)) which is a hand crafted rule, diminishes the credibility of the approach
    * **Limited Analysis of Transfer:** While the paper focuses on mitigating catastrophic forgetting, a more detailed analysis of forward and backward transfer would provide a more complete picture of the method's capabilities.
    * **Hyperparameter Tuning Details:** While the implementation details are provided, more information about the sensitivity of the method to hyperparameter settings (e.g., mrr, trr) and the tuning process would be beneficial.

* **Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of continual learning for LLMs. It introduces a novel, theoretically grounded, and empirically effective approach that addresses key limitations of existing methods. The method's ability to maintain model expressiveness, scale to long task sequences, and preserve general linguistic capabilities makes it a promising solution for real-world deployment scenarios. The research challenges a common assumption about parameter updates in continual learning by demonstrating full parameter updates without catastrophic forgetting. The work is likely to influence future research in this area, inspiring new methods that leverage geometric properties of weight matrices and adaptive subspace updates.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a strong contribution to the field of continual learning for LLMs, exhibiting novelty in its adaptive SVD approach and demonstrating compelling experimental results. The theoretical justification provides a robust foundation for the method. However, the computational cost associated with SVD, rank estimation sensitivity, and some lack of transfer analysis prevent it from receiving a higher score. While the idea of SVD being a proxy for the hessian seems plausible in principle, its implementation requires the manual tuning of a layer importance factor for balancing knowledge retention, which is a significant departure from the stated claim of mathematical elegance.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10
